## 368803: Connected domination conjecture for plane triangulations

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a plane triangulation of sufficiently large order $n$. A connected dominating set is a subset $X\subseteq V(G)$ such that $V(G)=\bigcup_{x\in X}N_G[x]$ and $G[X]$ is connected; let $\gamma_c(G)$ denote the minimum cardinality of a connected dominating set.

**Connected domination conjecture.**

$$
\gamma_c(G)\le\frac{3n}{8}.
$$

The paper constructs plane triangulations with $\gamma_c(G)\ge 3|V(G)|/8-1$, so this weaker bound is presented as a revised conjecture; the supplied status evidence says it remains open.

Primary locator: Revised connected-domination conjecture in conclusion

## 368804: Induced maximal outerplane subgraph conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a plane graph of sufficiently large order $n$. The outerplane subgraph number $s_{o'}(G)$ is the maximum order of an induced outerplane subgraph of $G$.

**Induced maximal outerplane subgraph conjecture.**

$$
s_{o'}(G)\ge\frac{5n}{8}.
$$

The construction in the paper gives examples with an induced maximal outerplane subgraph of order at most $5|V(G)|/8+1$; the supplied text does not state a resolution of this conjecture.

Primary locator: Revised induced outerplane conjecture in conclusion

## 368827: Cairns–Nikolayevsky thrackle conjecture for orientable surfaces

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $S_g$ be a compact orientable surface of genus $g>0$, and let $G$ be a graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges. **Cairns–Nikolayevsky's conjecture.** If $G$ can be thrackled on $S_g$, then

$$
m\le n+2g.
$$

Odd cycles and recursive constructions provide examples with $n+2g$ edges, motivating this proposed maximum. The paper states that this conjecture is disproved by constructions with substantially more edges, so the claim is not open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 2; Theorem 3

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

However, this statement is incorrect: as we illustrate in Figure 2, it is possible to thrackle  K_{3,3}  on  S_{1} .

As we also prove in that section, a construction used in the proof of Theorem 3 can be easily adapted to yield upper bounds that are roughly twice as large as these lower bounds.

921: Given a graph  G  let  {\mathcal{I}}(G)  denote the collection of all pairs of independent edges in  G . Using this notation, a simple drawing of  G  on some surface is a thrackle if and only if it has  |{\mathcal{I}}(G)|  crossings. Green and Ringeisen [11, Theorem 2] proved that for any integer  k ,  0\leq k\leq|{\mathcal{I}}(G)| , there is a simple drawing of  G  on some  S_{g}  with exactly  k  crossings. As noted in [11, Corollary 3], this implies in particular that every graph is thrackleable on some  S_{g} .
926: Green and Ringeisen gave no explicit upper bounds on  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(G)  in that paper, but Cottingham and Ringeisen derived upper bounds in a subsequent paper [6]. In a nutshell, Cottingham and Ringeisen noted that if  D  is a simple drawing of a graph  G  on some surface  S_{h} , and  e,f  are independent edges of  G  that do not cross in  D , then by adding at most two handles to  S_{h}  it is possible to modify  D  to obtain a drawing with the same crossings as  D  plus a crossing between  e  and  f . An iterative application of this observation yields that if  G  has a simple drawing on  S_{h}  with  c  crossings then  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(G)\leq h+2(|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|-c)  [11, Theorem 5.1].
936: As it happens, the arguments used by Green and Ringeisen in the proof of [11, Theorem 2] can be used to derive slightly better bounds. In their proof they note that one can consider a drawing as an embedding, regarding crossings as degree four vertices. If the drawing of a graph  G=(V,E)  under consideration is a thrackle, when regarded as an embedding it has  |V|+|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|  vertices,  |E|+2|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|  edges, and (obviously) at least one face. Euler’s formula then implies that the drawing can be hosted in  S_{g} , where  2-2g\geq|V|+|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|-(|E|+2|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|)+1 , that is, where  g\leq(1/2)(|E|-|V|+|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|+1) . Therefore  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(G)\leq(1/2)(|E|-|V|+|{\mathcal{I}}(G)|+1) .
941: Since  K_{m,n}  has  m+n  vertices,  mn  edges, and  |{\mathcal{I}}(K_{m,n})|=2\binom{m}{2}\binom{n}{2} , this last inequality implies that  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(K_{m,n})\leq m^{2}n^{2}/4+O(m^{2}n+mn^{2}) , and since  K_{n}  has  n  vertices,  \binom{n}{2}  edges and  |{\mathcal{I}}(K_{n})|=3\binom{n}{4} , it follows that  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(K_{n})\leq n^{4}/16+O(n^{3}) . Thus indeed this last argument yields better upper bounds than [11, Theorem 5.1].
1147: Cairns and Nikolayevsky proved that a bipartite graph  G  admits a generalized thrackle on an orientable surface  S  if and only if  G  can be embedded on  S  [3, Theorem 3]. This implies that if  G  is bipartite then its thrackle genus is at least its orientable genus  \gamma(G) . Ringel proved that  \gamma(K_{m,n})=(m-2)(n-2)/4  whenever  m\geq 2  and  n\geq 2  [18], and so it follows that  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(K_{m,n})\geq(m-2)(n-2)/4 .
1154: We finally note that another result by Cairns and Nikolayevsky (stated above as Theorem 11) implies the following lower bound on  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(K_{n}) . This shows that  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(K_{n})  is roughly at least half of the upper bound established in Corollary 19.
1178: Theorem 11 implies that if  G  is a connected graph with  n  vertices and  m  edges then  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(G)\geq m/4-n/2+1/2 . Therefore  {{\text{\rm tg}}}(K_{n})\geq\binom{n}{2}/4-n/2+1/2=(n^{2}-5n+4)/8 .
1212: Let  T  be a thrackle of  K_{2,n}  on  S_{g} . If we remove the vertices in the partite class of size  2 , we obtain a collection  {\mathscr{A}}  of simple arcs that pairwise intersect at exactly two points, hosted at the orientable surface of genus  g  punctured twice, where the endpoints of each arc in  {\mathscr{A}}  are the two punctures of the surface. It follows from a result of Cairns and Nikolayevsky [3, Lemma 5(a)] that the  n  arcs in  {\mathscr{A}}  are pairwise non-homologous mod 2, and so they are pairwise non-homotopic. By a result of Przytycki [17, Theorem 1.5] then it follows that  n=O(g^{3}) .

## 368830: Subdivision conjecture for percolated vertex expanders

Literal MathDB statement:

Fix $d\in\mathbb{N}$ and let $H$ be a graph of maximum degree $3$. Let $k\in\mathbb{N}$, and let $G$ be an $(=\hspace{-0.2em}k,d)$-vertex expander, meaning that every set $S\subseteq V(G)$ with $|S|=k$ has at least $dk$ external neighbors. For $\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_0(d)=d^{-O(1)}$, set $p=(1+\varepsilon)/d$ and let $G_p$ be the percolated graph. **Subdivision conjecture.** With high probability, $G_p$ contains a subdivision of $H$. This is proposed as a significant strengthening of the paper's main long-cycle theorem; the precise dependence of the threshold $\varepsilon_0(d)$ is part of the conjecture.

Primary locator: Conjecture 4.2

## 368831: The conjecture that bounded-above Lüroth sets with digits 3 and 4 cover modulo 1

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $L_{\leq k}$ denote the Lüroth set whose digits are at most $k$, for $k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}$. For subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, write $A+B=\{a+b:a\in A,b\in B\}$, and write $A\equiv\mathbb{R}\mod 1$ when $A+\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{R}$. **The conjecture.**

$$
L_{\leq 3}+L_{\leq 4}\equiv\mathbb{R}\mod 1.
$$

Theorem 1 establishes the analogous covering results for $L_{\leq 3}+L_{\leq 5}$, $L_{\leq 4}+L_{\leq 4}$, and three copies of $L_{\leq 3}$, but does not determine this mixed two-set case. Numerical clustering suggests that $L_{\leq 3}+L_{\leq 4}$ may contain an interval such as $[1,2]$.

512: Just as the times-3 map preserves the middle-thirds Cantor set, a more complicated map preserves continued fraction expansions with restricted partial quotients. The Gauss map (also known as the Gauss–Kuzmin–Wirsing operator) generates the continued fraction partial quotients and dynamically shifts the continued fraction expansions. Hence, the sets  F_{\leq k}  and  F_{\geq k}  are both invariant under this map. The Gauss map restricted to  F_{\leq k}  (or  F_{\geq k} ) models a symbolic dynamical system on the finite alphabet  \{1,2,\ldots,k\}  (or infinite alphabet  \{k,k+1,\ldots\} ). ## 368840: The even-hole-free 3-divisibility conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

A graph with at least one edge is **$k$-divisible** if the vertex set of each induced subgraph with at least one edge can be partitioned into $k$ sets, none of which contains a largest clique of that induced subgraph. An **even hole** is a chordless cycle of even length at least four.

**Even-hole-free 3-divisibility conjecture.** Every even-hole-free graph is 3-divisible.

This extends the paper's discussion of divisibility for even-hole-free graphs. The conjecture remains open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 4.8 and definition of k-divisibility

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

## 368872: Pseudo \(\ll\)-transitivity for finite semigroups of order at most eight

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $\ll$ be its relation as above. The semigroup $S$ is **pseudo $\ll$-transitive** if, whenever $u,s,t\in S$ satisfy $u\lll st$, $u\not\ll st$, and $(u,st)$ is $n$-essential, there exist elements $v_1,\ldots,v_n$ such that

$$
u\ll v_1\ll\cdots\ll v_n\ll st
$$

and the conditions defining pseudo $\ll$-transitivity hold. Here $\lll$ denotes the smallest partially ordered set containing $\ll$.

**Pseudo $\ll$-transitivity conjecture.** If

$$
|S|\leq 8,
$$

then $S$ is pseudo $\ll$-transitive.

This conjecture was verified by a C\# program for semigroups of order at most eight; no general proof or resolution beyond that computational verification is supplied here.

Primary locator: Conjecture 3.2; singleton-rich convention; pseudo relation definition

## 368889: Characterization of 2-distinguishable trees by branch orbit counts

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $T$ be a tree. For a vertex $w$ and a neighbor $x$ of $w$, let $T^x$ denote the component containing $x$ after deleting $w$, let $\mu(x)$ denote the number of isomorphism types among the branches rooted at $x$, and let $a(T^x)$ denote the number of distinguishing subsets of $T^x$. **Characterization conjecture.** The tree $T$ is $2$-distinguishable if and only if, for any vertex $w$ and any neighbor $x$ of $w$, $\mu(x)\leq a(T^x)$ if $T^x$ is finite, and

$$
\mu(x)=2^{|T^x|}
$$

if $T^x$ is infinite. This would characterize 2-distinguishable trees in terms of the number of branch types and distinguishing subsets of their rooted components, extending the preceding characterization of $a$-maximum trees; the paper provides no resolution of the conjecture.

Primary locator: Conjecture 10; Section 2; Theorem 8

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

## 368926: Ceiling-bound conjecture for the maximal Toeplitz number

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $n>1$, and let $\mathrm{Toep}_n$ be the maximum, over all $n\times n$ matrices, of the minimal number of Toeplitz matrices required to factor the matrix.

**Ceiling-bound conjecture.** For $n>1$,

$$
\mathrm{Toep}_n=\left\lceil\frac{n}{2}\right\rceil+1.
$$

The paper proposes this as a replacement for Ye and Lim's conjecture after disproving the floor-bound conjecture in dimension $n=3$. It is presented as a reasonable substitution, and no resolution is supplied.

Primary locator: Conjecture 5.3

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

271: Theorem 1.1 (Ye-Lim).
311: It seems relevant to remark that the bound on  \mathrm{Toep}_{n}  is clearly not exactly equal to its upper bound in all cases, since for  n=1 , every matrix is Toeplitz. The same applies for the bound for invertible matrices. For  n=2 , one can solve the problem of factorizing any matrix with two Toeplitz factors as an easy exercise. This means that the problem trully starts at  n=3 . Ye and Lim made the reasonable conjecture that their generic bound was true for any matrix, following the pattern observed for  n=1,2  (see Conjecture 1 in [12]).
315: Conjecture 1.2 (Ye-Lim).

It can be interpreted as well in terms of the boundary values of the function belonging to  L^{2}(\mathbb{T}) . It is one of the most studied objects in the complex analysis of the last century. This is in part thanks to a Theorem of Beurling that provides a complete understanding of the invariant subspaces for the shift operator acting there (and as a consequence, obtaining a complete characterization of cyclic functions). Invariant subspaces are, in this context, directly related with so-called inner functions, while cyclic functions are those called outer. Every  f\in H^{2}  can be factorized as  f=IE  where  I  is inner,  E  is outer and this provides all information about which invariant subspaces contain the function  f . See [8] for more on the basics of  H^{2} .

## 368945: The ODP conjecture on the second largest local volume of klt singularities

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $x\in X$ be an $n$-dimensional klt singularity, and let $\widehat{\operatorname{vol}}(x,X)$ denote its local volume, defined as the infimum of the normalized volumes of valuations centered at $x$. An **ordinary double point** is a klt singularity analytically equivalent to a quadratic hypersurface singularity of ordinary double-point type. **ODP conjecture.** The second largest local volume of an $n$-dimensional klt singularity is

$$
2(n-1)^n.
$$

Moreover,

$$
\widehat{\operatorname{vol}}(x,X)=2(n-1)^n
$$

if and only if $x\in X$ is an ordinary double point. The maximal local volume is known to be $n^n$, attained exactly at smooth points; this conjecture predicts the next possible value and characterizes the singularities attaining it.

Primary locator: Conjecture 2.3

## 368988: Waldschmidt's density conjecture for rational points on abelian varieties

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $\mathcal{A}$ be a simple abelian variety of dimension $g$ over a number field $K$ embedded in $\mathbb{R}$. Let $\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{R})^0$ be the identity component, let $\hat{h}$ be the canonical height, and define

$$
\hat{\eta}_{\mathcal{A}}(h)=\inf\left\{\delta:\text{ for every }\zeta\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{R})^0\text{ there exists }\gamma\in\mathcal{A}(K)\text{ with }\hat{h}(\gamma)\leq h\text{ and }\operatorname{dist}(\zeta,\gamma)<\delta\right\}.
$$

Denote by $r$ the rank of the Mordell--Weil group $\mathcal{A}(K)$. **Waldschmidt's conjecture.** For every $\varepsilon>0$, there exists $h_0>0$, depending only on $\mathcal{A}$, $K$, and $\varepsilon$, such that for every $h\geq h_0$,

$$
\hat{\eta}_{\mathcal{A}}(h)\leq h^{-(r/2g)+\varepsilon}.
$$

This conjecture predicts the optimal rate at which points of bounded canonical height from $\mathcal{A}(K)$ approximate the real identity component, with the exponent governed by the Mordell--Weil rank. The paper relates it to a matrix-approximation property, but does not establish the conjecture in general.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1.4; definition 1.3

## 368991: Near-maximum-degree realizable exponents conjecture for graphs

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $H$ be a graph, with $v(H)$ vertices, $e(H)$ edges, and maximum degree $\Delta(H)$. A rational number is realizable for $H$ if it occurs as an exponent in the generalized Turán-counting function for $H$. **Near-maximum-degree realizable exponents conjecture.** Every rational number in the interval

$$
\left[v(H)-\frac{e(H)}{\Delta(H)},v(H)\right]
$$

is realizable for $H$.
This is presented as a slight weakening of the preceding independence-number question. The supplied text gives no resolution of this claim, so its status remains open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 6.3; generalized Turan definitions

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

243: The behavior of  \mathrm{ex}(n,\mathcal{F})  is well-understood whenever  \mathcal{F}  contains only non-bipartite graphs due to the famed Erdős-Stone-Simonovits Theorem [10], but the behavior of  \mathrm{ex}(n,\mathcal{F})  when  \mathcal{F}  contains a bipartite graph is far from clear despite decades of active research into the problem, and we refer the reader to the survey [13] for a more thorough summary of what is known in the area.
252: Theorem 1.1 ([4]).
262: Theorem 1.1 is a highly influential result, with it serving as the catalyst for a large body of literature dedicated towards proving special cases of the rational exponents conjecture [21]. It is also worth mentioning that Theorem 1.1 was proven using the powerful random polynomial method introduced by Bukh [3], with this method having since been further developed to solve a large number of problems in combinatorics, see e.g. [5, 7, 8, 11, 19, 24].
272: Motivated by Theorem 1.1, we consider the following.

It is natural to ask to what extent this holds for other  H , and in particular what happens for families of  H  which contain  K_{2}  as a member. For example, one might ask what happens when  H  is a clique, with this case being solved in full by English, Halfpap, and Krueger [9].

However,  K_{1,1,t}  can be viewed as the rooted power of  (K_{3},\{x,y\})  with  xy  an edge of the triangle, which has rooted density 2. Thus when applying Theorem 3.2 to avoid a family containing large powers of  (K_{3},\{x,y\}) , the number of copies of  K_{1,1,2}  that one finds is at most on the order of  n^{4-5/2}=n^{1.5} . As such, a direct application of random polynomials can not be used to prove the existence of realizable exponents in  (1.5,2) . Nevertheless, it is in fact possible to show that every rational in  (1.5,2)  is realizable for  H . On the other hand, we must have  |L|=O(n^{1-\varepsilon/2})  since we know  e(G)\leq\mathrm{ex}(n,\mathcal{F})=O(n^{2-1/d}) , so again being  K_{2,q} -free implies that the number of copies of  C_{4}  using only vertices in  L  is at most  q|L|^{2}=O(n^{2-\varepsilon})  as desired.

## 368992: Gyárfás' boundedness conjecture for graphs with odd-cycle chromatic number at most three

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a graph, and let $r(G)$ denote the maximum chromatic number of a subgraph spanned by an odd cycle of $G$, while $\chi(G)$ denotes the chromatic number of $G$. **Gyárfás' boundedness conjecture.** There exists a constant $k$ (perhaps $k=4$) such that if $G$ is a graph with $r(G)\leq 3$, then $\chi(G)\leq k$. This is the main problem discussed in the paper and is attributed to Gyárfás; the best progress cited is the logarithmic upper bound of Randerath and Schiermeyer, and the conjecture remains open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1.1

## 368993: Conjecture that P₅-free graphs are path-perfect

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a graph. A graph is **path-perfect** if its chromatic number equals the maximum chromatic number of a subgraph spanned by an odd cycle, that is, $\chi(G)=r(G)$. **P₅-free path-perfectness conjecture.** If $G$ is a $P_5$-free graph, then $G$ is path-perfect. The source describes this as an even stronger conjecture than path-perfectness for the two preceding forbidden-subgraph classes; it remains open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 7.4

## 369043: Zhao's conjecture on the short zero-sum threshold below the Davenport midpoint

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a finite abelian group with rank $r(G)\geq 2$ and $\mathsf{D}(G)=\mathsf{D}^*(G)$, and let $\exp(G)$ denote its exponent. Let $\mathsf{s}_{\leq m}(G)$ be the smallest length such that every sequence over $G$ has a nonempty zero-sum subsequence of length at most $m$. **Zhao's conjecture.** If $G\not\cong C_2^4$, $\mathsf{D}(G)-2\geq\exp(G)$, and $\exp(G)<(\mathsf{D}(G)-1)/2$, then 
$$
\mathsf{s}_{\leq \mathsf{D}(G)-2}(G)=\mathsf{D}(G)+1.
$$
 This refines the expected behavior of the bounded-length zero-sum invariant in a range below the midpoint of the Davenport constant.

Primary locator: Conjecture 6.1; Theorem 1.11 proof; Lemmas 3.6-3.7

## 369044: Zhao's midpoint conjecture for bounded-length zero-sum constants

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a finite abelian group with rank $r(G)\geq 2$ and $\mathsf{D}(G)=\mathsf{D}^*(G)$. Let $k$ be a positive integer with $\mathsf{D}(G)-k\in[\exp(G),\mathsf{D}(G)]$. Let $\mathsf{s}_{\leq m}(G)$ denote the smallest length such that every sequence over $G$ has a nonempty zero-sum subsequence of length at most $m$. **Zhao's midpoint conjecture.** If $\mathsf{D}(G)-k\geq(\mathsf{D}(G)+1)/2$, then 
$$
\mathsf{s}_{\leq \mathsf{D}(G)-k}(G)\leq\mathsf{D}(G)+k.
$$
 If $\mathsf{D}(G)-k<(\mathsf{D}(G)+1)/2$, then 
$$
\mathsf{s}_{\leq \mathsf{D}(G)-k}(G)>\mathsf{D}(G)+k.
$$
 The paper notes that this holds for $G=C_3^3$ and $C_5^3$, and that the first assertion is known at the endpoint $\mathsf{D}(G)-k=\exp(G)$ for finite abelian $p$-groups.

Primary locator: Conjecture 6.2; Theorems 1.12-1.13

## 369045: Zhao's conjecture on the midpoint bound for exact-length zero-sum constants

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a finite abelian group, let $\exp(G)$ denote its exponent, and let $\mathsf{D}(G)$ denote its Davenport constant. Let $\mathsf{s}_{k\exp(G)}(G)$ be the smallest length forcing a zero-sum subsequence of length $k\exp(G)$. **Zhao's conjecture.** If $k\exp(G)\in[(\mathsf{D}(G)+1)/2,\mathsf{D}(G)]$, then 
$$
\mathsf{s}_{k\exp(G)}(G)\leq 2\mathsf{D}(G)-1.
$$
 If $k\exp(G)<(\mathsf{D}(G)+1)/2$, then 
$$
\mathsf{s}_{k\exp(G)}(G)>2\mathsf{D}(G)-1.
$$
 This conjecture proposes a sharp midpoint transition for the exact-length zero-sum invariant, building on the preceding bounded-length conjectures and known lower bounds.

Primary locator: Conjecture 6.4 and final paragraph of Section 6

## 369063: Kasteleyn's Bunkbed Conjecture for Bernoulli bond percolation

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G=(V,E)$ be an undirected graph, and let $(V^-,E^-)$ and $(V^+,E^+)$ be two copies of $G$. For each $v\in V$, write $v^-$ and $v^+$ for its copies, and form the bunkbed graph by adding bidirected vertical edges between $v^-$ and $v^+$ for every $v\in V$. Under $p$-Bernoulli bond percolation, let $x\leftrightarrow y$ denote the event that the percolated bunkbed graph contains a path connecting $x$ and $y$. **Kasteleyn's Bunkbed Conjecture.** For every undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ and every $u,v\in V$, 

$$
\mathbb{P}_p(u^-\leftrightarrow v^-) \geq \mathbb{P}_p(u^-\leftrightarrow v^+).
$$

The conjecture asserts that the two vertices on the same floor should be at least as likely to be connected as vertices on opposite floors. It was disproved by Gladkov, Pak and Zimin, whose counterexample was based on a graph found by Hollom.

Primary locator: Introduction and bunkbed definition

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

194: We construct a simple acyclic directed graph for which the Bunkbed Conjecture is false, thereby resolving conjectures posed by Leander and by Hollom.
204: The Bunkbed Conjecture, introduced by Kasteleyn in 1985 [9, Remark 5], postulates a certain monotonicity of connectivity probabilities in Bernoulli bond percolation on an undirected graph. Being an intuitive, but surprisingly hard to prove, statement, a lot of effort has been put in proving its correctness. Despite that, the original conjecture turned out to be false, as shown recently by Gladkov, Pak and Zimin [2] who built on the work of Hollom disproving the hypergraph variant [4].
210: One of the remaining promising general models considered is the setup of acyclic directed graphs put forward by Leander [5], and highlighted by Hollom [4]. Nevertheless, in this work we prove that the Bunkbed Conjecture does not hold in this case either.
225: The original Bunkbed Conjecture states the following:
229: Conjecture 1.1.
248: This conjecture was disproved by Gladkov, Pak and Zimin [2]. Their counterexample is ingeniously based on a graph found by Hollom [4], which in turn served as a counterexample to the Hypergraph Bunkbed Conjecture. The apparent intuition behind the conjecture is that vertices  u^{-}  and  v^{-}  are closer to each other than  u^{-}  and  v^{+}  for any conceivable notion of closeness, and so the chance of an open path between  u^{-}  and  v^{-}  should be at least as large as the chance of open path between  u^{-}  and  v^{+} . There are certain instances where the conjecture does hold, notably on complete graphs [1, 10], for adjacent vertices in edge-transitive graphs [8], for vertices  u ,  v  with the same neighbourhood [8]; and also for the random cluster model with parameter  q=2  [3]; for more thorough discussion see [2].
253: Leander considered the Bunkbed Conjecture for directed graphs in [5], in which case one compares the probability of existence of directed paths in a Bernoulli percolated bunkbed graph. This directed case was disproved by Hollom, who found a directed multigraph counterexample. Hollom conjectured that one can avoid the use of multiple edges. Further, Leander considered the conjecture for acyclic directed graphs. Interestingly, Hollom’s counterexample contains a lot of directed cycles and as such does not disprove Leander’s acyclic variant. The acyclic graphs seem promising to satisfy the conjecture as they might be amenable to some inductive argument. Despite that, we find a simple acyclic directed graph not satisfying the conjecture, thereby resolving the conjectures by Leander and by Hollom.
257: Theorem 1.2.
279: The proof of Theorem 1.2 has two steps. Firstly, in Section 3 we prove that bond percolation on  \overline{G_{1}}  conditioned to have vertical edges  (2^{-},2^{+}),(5^{-},5^{+}),(8^{-},8^{+})  does not satisfy the Bunkbed Conjecture. Then, in Section 4, we show that in unconditioned bond percolation on  \overline{G_{2}^{k}}  the gadgets behave with high probability as if they were vertical edges at  2 ,  5 ,  8  in  \overline{G_{1}} .
347: Definition 3.1 (Conditioned).
360: The Bunkbed Conjecture does not hold for the conditioned bunkbed model  \mathcal{E}_{T}  of  G_{1}  with  T=\{2,5,8\} , and  u=1 ,  v=9 .
622: Definition 4.1.
632: We boost the conditioned counterexample to an unconditioned one, thereby proving Theorem 1.2. In order to do so, we substitute in the graph  G_{1}  each of the vertices  i=2,5,8  with a gadget on  k+2  vertices  i_{a},i_{b},i_{1},\ldots,i_{k} , as shown in Figure 3. We call the resulting graph  G_{2}^{k} .
636: Proof of Theorem 1.2.

## 369073: Restricted antidirected subdivision conjecture for oriented graphs

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $D$ be an oriented graph, and let $\delta(D)$ denote its minimum degree. For positive integers $r$ and $s$, write $\vec{K}_{s,s}$ for the consistently oriented complete bipartite graph, and call an antidirected subdivision of a graph **restricted** when all its branch vertices are sources.

**Restricted antidirected subdivision conjecture.** Every oriented graph $D$ with

$$
\delta(D) \geq f_{}(2s,f_{}(r,s))
$$

contains a copy of $\vec{K}_{s,s}$ or an induced restricted antidirected subdivision of $K_r$.

This conjecture proposes a more precise version of the preceding minimum-degree result for oriented graphs. The supplied text does not indicate whether it has been resolved.

Primary locator: Conjecture 3.2; Theorem 1.2 and K-O threshold

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

175: 3.3 Analogues of Gyárfás-Sumner’s Conjecture
252: Theorem 1.1.
262: Our proof shows that  f_{\ref{thm:main}}(2,d)  can be taken to be at most triple exponential in  d , which is certainly not tight. Observe that, in the case of  G  being a complete graph, Theorem 1.1 implies Ramsey’s theorem, hence showing that  f_{\ref{thm:main}}(2,d)  must be at least as large as the diagonal Ramsey number  R(d+1,d+1) , which is at least  2^{d/2}  [19].
267: Combined with a recent result of Kwan et al. [33] (see Theorem 2.11 below), Theorem 1.1 implies the following for oriented graphs. An oriented graph is a digraph with at most one arc between any pair of vertices. It is antidirected if all its vertices are either sources or sinks. The minimum degree of an oriented graph is the minimum degree of its underlying graph. The transitive tournament of order  r , denoted by  TT_{r} , is the unique acyclic orientation of  K_{r} .
271: Theorem 1.2.
281: Using Theorems 1.1 and 1.2, we show that certain extensions of degree-bounded graph classes preserve degree-boundedness. We begin with some definitions.
298: The following is a direct consequence of Theorem 1.1.
323: The following is a direct consequence of Theorem 1.2.
347: After recalling some notation and preliminary results in Section 2, we discuss applications of Corollaries 1.3 and 1.4 in Section 3, and propose a few further research directions along the way. Proofs of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 are postponed to Section 4.
404: Our proof of Theorem 1.1 contains probabilistic arguments. We make use of the following two well-known bounds.
452: Theorem 2.4 (Kühn and Osthus [34]).
462: The fact that, in Theorem 2.4, the induced subdivision of  K_{r}  can be chosen to be even is not explicitly stated in [34]. To see this, note that it is proved [34, Theorem 1.2] that every graph with sufficiently large minimum degree contains  K_{s,s}  or an induced  1 -subdivision of a graph  H  with large minimum degree, where a  1 -subdivision is a subdivision in which all edges are subdivided exactly once. Combined with a result of Mader [35] (see also [15, Theorem 3.5.1]) stating that every graph with sufficiently large minimum degree contains a subdivision of  K_{r} , we indeed obtain Theorem 2.4.

## 369174: Aharoni's rainbow Caccetta–Häggkvist conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be an $n$-vertex graph whose edges are colored with $n$ colors, and let the **rainbow girth** be the minimum length of a rainbow cycle, with value $infty$ if no rainbow cycle exists. Assume that every color class has size at least $r$. **Aharoni's rainbow Caccetta–Häggkvist conjecture.** The rainbow girth of $G$ is at most

$$
\left\lceil\frac{n}{r}\right\rceil.
$$

This conjecture generalizes the Caccetta–Häggkvist conjecture to edge-colored graphs. The supplied text does not state whether it has been resolved; the surrounding paper studies sufficient conditions for logarithmic rainbow girth.

Primary locator: Aharoni conjecture in introduction

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

## 369259: Higher-uniformity monochromatic clique bound for k-partite colorings

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $g_k(n)$ be the maximum number of edges in an $n$-vertex iterated blowup of a $k$-uniform edge. A **$k$-partite coloring** of the complete graph $K_n$ is a coloring arising from a partition into $k$ parts as in the source. **Higher-uniformity conjecture.** For $k\in\{4,5,6\}$, every $k$-partite coloring of $K_n$ has at most $g_k(n)$ monochromatic copies of $K_k$.

The conjecture seeks to extend the corresponding results proved in the paper for lower uniformities to $k=4,5,6$.

Primary locator: Conjecture 6.3; Section 2.1; Section 5 proof

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

257: Definition 1.1.
285: Conjecture 1.2 (Erdős and Hajnal [11]).
305: little progress was made on the problem until 2010, when Conlon, Fox, and Sudakov [8] proved Conjecture 1.2 for infinitely many  s  when  k=3 .
307: In 2021, Mubayi and Razborov [15] proved Conjecture 1.2 for all  s>k\geq 4  by reducing the conjecture to a certain combinatorial optimization problem about inducibility of rainbow tournaments. Their techniques become stronger as the uniformity  k  grows larger, and the case  k=4  required the use of computer verification. For the unsolved case  k=3 , they noted that “many crucial calculations in this paper completely fall apart.”

We reduce Theorem 1.3 to a Turán-type problem about tightly connected hypergraphs, which have recently been the object of intense study. Breakthrough results of Balogh and Luo [2] and Lidický, Mattes, and Pfender [13] established the Turán density of the  3 -uniform tight cycle minus an edge, while Kamčev, Letzter, and Pokrovskiy [12] and Bodnár, León, Liu, and Pikhurko [3] determined the Turán density of the tight cycle.

To our knowledge, this is one of the first nontrivial hypergraph problems to be solved exactly where the extremizer is an iterated blowup. The interested reader should compare this to the results of Balogh and Luo [2] and Lidický, Mattes, and Pfender [13], which imply that for a certain  H\in\mathcal{T}_{3} ,  \textnormal{ex}(n,H)=(1+o(1))g_{3}(n) .

## 369260: Jiang–Tidor–Yao–Zhang–Zhao bounded-error conjecture for equiangular lines

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $\alpha\in(0,1)$, define

$$
\lambda=\frac{1-\alpha}{2\alpha},
$$

and let $\kappa=\kappa(\lambda)$ be the spectral radius order of $\lambda$, namely the least number of vertices of a graph whose largest adjacency-matrix eigenvalue is $\lambda$, or $\infty$ if no such graph exists. **Jiang–Tidor–Yao–Zhang–Zhao conjecture.** If $\kappa=\infty$, then

$$
N_{\alpha}(d)=d+O_{\alpha}(1).
$$

The source states that this remains open to determine the set of $\lambda$ for which the conjecture holds, although it has been confirmed when $\lambda$ is not a totally real algebraic integer.

Primary locator: Source Conjecture 1.1; Schildkraut Theorem 1.4

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

175: 4 Proof of Theorem 
177: 5 Proof of Theorem 
224: For given  \Delta>0  and  0<\lambda<3/\sqrt{2} , we show that the maximum multiplicity that  \lambda  can appear as the second largest eigenvalue of a connected graph with maximum degree at most  \Delta  is  O_{\Delta,\lambda}(1) . This result answers a question due to Jiang, Tidor, Yao, Zhang and Zhao [Question 6.4, Ann. of Math. (2) 194 (2021), no. 3, 729-743] in the case of  0<\lambda<3/\sqrt{2} , and consequently leads to improvements in their results on equiangular lines. Our proof is based on the concept of nodal domains of eigenfunctions. Indeed, we establish a multiplicity estimate in terms of maximum degree and cyclomatic number of the graph, via a novel construction of eigenfunctions with large number of nodal domains.
245: For  \alpha\in(0,1) , let  N_{\alpha}(d)  be the maximum number of equiangular lines in  \mathbb{R}^{d}  with fixed angle  \arccos\alpha . In 1973, Lemmens and Seidel [17] posed the problem of determining  N_{\alpha}(d) . In fact, they proved that  N_{\frac{1}{3}}(d)=2d-2  for  d\geq 15 . Furthermore, they conjectured [17, Conjecture 5.8] that  N_{1/5}(d)=\left\lfloor\frac{3(d-1)}{2}\right\rfloor  for  d\geq 185 , and  N_{1/5}(d)=276  for  23\leq d\leq 185 . Neumaier [20] proved in 1989 that  N_{1/5}(d)  equals  \left\lfloor\frac{3(d-1)}{2}\right\rfloor  for sufficiently large  d . In 2022, Cao, Koolen, Lin, and Yu [7] confirmed this conjecture completely.

403: The proof of Theorem 3 is based on the concept of nodal domains. The idea of deriving upper bounds of eigenvalue multiplicity using nodal domains dates back to Cheng’s pioneering work [8] in the context of Riemann surfaces. Recall that Cheng’s multiplicity estimate is given in terms of the genus of the Riemann surface. Van Der Holst [22] extended Cheng’s approach to graphs, proving that the multiplicity of the second smallest Laplacian eigenvalue of a maximal planar graph is at most  3 . Following Cheng’s idea, Lin, Lippner, Mangoubi, and Yau [19] derived
409: Unlike the previous extensions of Cheng’s idea in terms of genus, we show a multiplicity estimate in terms of the cyclomatic number of a graph (see Lemma 3), via a novel construction of eigenfunctions with a large number of nodal domains. Furthermore, we show that, for a graph  G  with  \lambda_{2}(G)<\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}} , the cyclomatic number of  G  is small after removing a vertex subset, whose size is bounded by a constant depending only on  \lambda_{2}(G) . Then we finish the proof of Theorem 3 by Cauchy’s Interlace Theorem.

## 369264: Convergence of expanding twist-torus measures for Veech surfaces

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $(M,\omega)$ be a horizontally periodic Veech surface, let $\mathcal{M}=\overline{\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})\cdot\mathbb{T}(\omega)}$, and let $\mu_\mathbb{T}$ be a fully supported Lebesgue probability measure on $\mathbb{T}(\omega)$. Assume that a pseudo-Anosov element of $\mathrm{Aff}^+(M,\omega)$ acts as the identity matrix on $\mathrm{Cyl}^0(\omega)$. Let $\mu_\mathcal{M}$ be the unique $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})$-ergodic probability measure fully supported on $\mathcal{M}$. **Convergence conjecture.** Then

$$
(g_t)_*\mu_\mathbb{T}\longrightarrow\mu_\mathcal{M}\quad\text{as }t\rightarrow\infty.
$$

The preceding theorem gives full support for every weak-$*$ limit under the stated hypothesis, and the conjecture predicts uniqueness of the limiting measure and convergence of the entire family. It is motivated by an affirmative answer to the cited problem for Veech surfaces satisfying the pseudo-Anosov identity hypothesis, but the statement remains open in the source.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1.5 and Theorem B

## 369269: Finiteness conjecture for equal radicals of consecutive products

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $k$ and $\ell$ be integers greater than $1$, and let $m$ and $n$ be integers. Define

$$
R_{k}(m)=\operatorname{rad}\bigl(m(m+1)\cdots(m+k-1)\bigr),
$$

where $\operatorname{rad}(r)$ is the largest squarefree divisor of $r$. **Finiteness conjecture.** If $(k,\ell)\neq(2,2)$, then the equation

$$
\operatorname{rad}\bigl(m(m+1)\cdots(m+k-1)\bigr)=\operatorname{rad}\bigl(n(n+1)\cdots(n+\ell-1)\bigr)
$$

has only finitely many solutions. The conjecture concerns the expected rarity of pairs of consecutive products with equal radicals; the exceptional case $(k,\ell)=(2,2)$ is explicitly excluded, and the source gives no resolution.

Primary locator: Conjecture 11; definition of F_(k,l)

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

198: Let  (m,n,k)  be a tuple of integers with the property that if  i\leq k , then  m+i  and  n+i  have the same radical. Using a result on the abc Conjecture, we bound  k  from above, improving a result of Balasubramanian, Shorey, and Waldschmidt. We also bound the number of pairs  (m,n)  for which  m<n\leq x  and  m(m+1)\cdots(m+k-1))  and  n(n+1)\cdots(n+\ell-1)  have the same radical and the number of pairs for which  m+i  and  n+i  have the same radical for all  i<k .

## 369276: Kawarabayashi–Picasarri-Arrieta Reed-type conjecture for digraphs

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $D$ be a digraph. Define its maximum geometric-mean degree by

$$
\widetilde{\Delta}(D)=\max_{v\in V(D)}\sqrt{d^-(v)d^+(v)},
$$

let $\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\omega}(D)$ be its biclique number, and let $\vec{\chi}(D)$ be its dichromatic number. **Kawarabayashi–Picasarri-Arrieta conjecture.**

$$
\vec{\chi}(D) \leq \left\lceil \frac{1}{2}\big(\widetilde{\Delta}(D)+1+\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\omega}(D)\big)\right\rceil.
$$

This is a directed analogue of Reed's conjecture. The source records an intermediate result with an error parameter, but does not state that the conjecture itself is resolved.

Primary locator: Conjecture 5

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

167: 2.3 The Directed Brooks’ Theorem
172: 4 Proof of Theorem 
271: The relationships between the chromatic number  \chi(G) , the clique number  \omega(G) , and the maximum degree  \Delta(G)  of a graph  G  have attracted a lot of attention during the last decades. Perhaps the first non-trivial result here is Brooks’ Theorem [14], which states that, for a connected graph  G ,  \chi(G)  is at most  \Delta(G) , unless  G  is an odd cycle or a complete graph. Following this, in 1977, Borodin and Kostochka [13] made the following well-known conjecture, which, if true, would give a nice extension of Brooks’ theorem for graphs with maximum degree at least  9 .
275: Conjecture 1 (Borodin–Kostochka [13]).
290: Conjecture 2 (Reed [38]).
301: If true, it implies in particular that every graph  G  with chromatic number at least  \Delta(G)  contains a clique of size  \Delta(G)-2 , thereby approaching Conjecture 1.
306: Theorem 3 ([38]).
323: Theorem 3 implies the weaker version of Borodin–Kostochka’s Conjecture stating that every graph  G  with chromatic number at least  \Delta(G)  contains a clique of size  \Delta(G)-C , for some absolute constant  C . One year later, Reed [39] strengthened this by proving that Conjecture 1 holds for large values of  \Delta .
327: Theorem 4 ([39]).
342: The purpose of this work is to provide extensions of Conjecture 1 and Theorem 4 to the digraph setting. We thus introduce three digraph parameters extending  \Delta ,  \omega , and  \chi . By an extension, we mean that if  D  is symmetric, that is,  D  is obtained from a graph  G  by replacing each edge by two opposite arcs, then these digraph parameters on  D  coincide with  \Delta ,  \omega , and  \chi  on  G .
358: For instance, the Strong Perfect Graph Theorem [8], Gyárfás–Sumner’s conjecture [5, 3, 16], Hadwiger’s Conjecture [9, 28], and Kostochka–Yancey’s type of bounds [27, 4, 6, 24, 37] have been generalised to digraphs via the notion of dicolouring.

Intuitively, if the maximum in-degree of a digraph is arbitrarily large compared to its maximum out-degree, one cannot expect to apply Lovász Local Lemma, as it seems unlikely to bound the degree of “bad events”. That is why finding upper bounds on the dichromatic number of a digraph  D  involving  \Delta^{+}(D)  is usually significantly harder than ones involving  \tilde{\Delta}(D) . ## 369278: Directed Borodin–Kostochka conjecture for maximum out-degree

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $D$ be a digraph with maximum out-degree $\Delta^+(D)$, biclique number $\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\omega}(D)$, and dichromatic number $\vec{\chi}(D)$. Write $\vec{C_3}\boxplus \overleftrightarrow{K}_{\Delta-2}$ for the directed-cycle obstruction described above. **Maximum-out-degree directed Borodin–Kostochka conjecture.** If $\Delta^+(D)=\Delta\geq 9$ and $\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\omega}(D)\leq\Delta-1$, then

$$
\vec{\chi}(D)\leq\Delta-1
$$

unless $D$ contains $\vec{C_3}\boxplus \overleftrightarrow{K}_{\Delta-2}$. This conjecture is presented as a stronger-parameter analogue of the preceding directed conjecture; the source proves the corresponding result for sufficiently large maximum out-degree but leaves the conjecture open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 11; Theorem 12

545: Furthermore, when only the out-degree of a digraph is bounded, probabilistic methods are unlikely to work. Intuitively, if the maximum in-degree of a digraph is arbitrarily large compared to its maximum out-degree, one cannot expect to apply Lovász Local Lemma, as it seems unlikely to bound the degree of “bad events”. That is why finding upper bounds on the dichromatic number of a digraph  D  involving  \Delta^{+}(D)  is usually significantly harder than ones involving  \tilde{\Delta}(D) . The most emblematic open problem of this kind is probably the following one, proposed by Kawarabayashi and Picasarri-Arrieta [26], whose version with  \tilde{\Delta}  has been proved first by Harutyunyan and Mohar [23] (see also [20]), and appears to be a particular case of Theorem 6.

## 369284: Leaf-to-leaf path length conjecture for trees with a given degree sequence

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $T$ be a tree with degree sequence $s$, and suppose that $T$ has no vertex of degree $2$. Let $lp(T)$ denote the number of different lengths of leaf-to-leaf paths in $T$, and let ${\rm rad}(s)$ denote the minimum radius of a tree with degree sequence $s$.

**Leaf-to-leaf path length conjecture.**

$$
lp(T)\geq {\rm rad}(s)-O(1).
$$

This conjecture seeks a lower bound on the number of distinct leaf-to-leaf path lengths determined by the degree sequence, strengthening bounds in terms of the number of leaves and maximum degree. The paper proves a weaker bound with a logarithmic error term, namely $lp(T)\geq {\rm rad}(s)-\log_2({\rm rad}(s))$, while the stated bounded-error improvement remains open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 2

## 369315: The Igusa-function formula conjecture for the middle symplectic local zeta function

Literal MathDB statement:

For a positive integer $n$, let $q$ be the residue-field cardinality and let $s$ be a complex variable. Let $\mathcal{Z}_{n,n,\mathfrak{o}}^{\mathsf{C}}(s)$ denote the symplectic local zeta function at the middle parameter $\ell=n$, and let

$$
\operatorname{I}_n(Y;X_1,\dots,X_n)=\sum_{I\subseteq[n]}\binom{n}{I}_Y\prod_{i\in I}\frac{X_i}{1-X_i}
$$

be the Igusa function of degree $n$. **The Igusa-function formula conjecture.** One has

$$
\mathcal{Z}_{n,n,\mathfrak{o}}^{\mathsf{C}}(s)=\frac{1}{1-q^{\binom{n+1}{2}-ns}}\operatorname{I}_n\left(q^{-1};\left(q^{\binom{n+1}{2}-\binom{i}{2}-ns}\right)_{i\in[n]}\right).
$$

This is motivated by the corresponding established formula for $\ell=0$ and by computations for $n\leqslant 13$; the claimed identity for the middle value $\ell=n$ remains conjectural in the supplied text.

Primary locator: Conjecture 7.6; symplectic local zeta definitions

## 369345: Eulerian odd-cover conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be an Eulerian graph, let $\Delta(G)$ be its maximum degree, and let a **path odd-cover** (respectively, **cycle odd-cover**) be a collection of paths (respectively, cycles) whose symmetric difference is $G$. **Eulerian odd-cover conjecture.** There exists a constant $c$ such that $G$ admits a path odd-cover of size

$$
\frac{1}{2}\Delta(G)+c
$$

and a cycle odd-cover of size at most

$$
\frac{1}{2}\Delta(G)+c.
$$

The paper proves upper bounds of $\lceil3\Delta(G)/4\rceil$ for both types of odd-cover, whereas the trivial lower bound is approximately $\Delta(G)/2$; the conjecture proposes that only an additive constant separates the optimum from this lower bound.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1.6; Section 1.2

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

Unfortunately, this statement is not true, as we will demonstrate with an explicit example in Section 3.2. Thus, new techniques will be needed to resolve Conjecture 1.3.
459: Another interesting observation in [7] is that path odd-covers are strengthenings of linear forest decompositions (recall that a linear forest is a graph whose connected components are all paths). The linear arboricity of a graph  G , denoted  \mathrm{la}(G) , is the minimum cardinality of a linear forest decomposition of  G . If  \mathcal{H}=\{P_{1},,\ldots,P_{k}\}  is a path odd-cover of  G , then  G  also admits a linear forest decomposition of the same cardinality  k , obtained by replacing each path  P_{i}  in  \mathcal{H}  by the linear forest  (P_{i}\cap G)\setminus(P_{1}\cup\cdots\cup P_{i-1}) . Thus the minimum size of a path odd-cover of  G  is at least  \mathrm{la}(G) . The authors of [7] proved a partial converse of this statement for Eulerian graphs: for every Eulerian graph  G\subseteq K_{n} , there exists  n^{\prime}\geq n  such that  G  admits a path odd-cover in  K_{n^{\prime}}  of size  \mathrm{la}(G)  (and also a cycle odd-cover in  K_{n^{\prime}}  of size at most  \mathrm{la}(G) ). These observations connect linear arboricity to path and cycle odd-covers, which are in turn related to diameters of partition polytopes. In this work, we strengthen these connections by refining a proof of Akiyama, Exoo, and Harary [1] that every graph  G  with  \Delta(G)=4  has linear arboricity at most 3 to obtain the following result on path and cycle odd-covers; our proof of Theorem 1.2 is also based on this proof of [1].

Hence, for graphs with many vertices with odd degree (more precisely, for graphs  G  with  \frac{v_{\mathrm{odd}}(G)}{2}\geq\Delta_{e}(G) ), the minimum size of a path odd-cover of  G  is equal to the trivial lower bound  \frac{v_{\mathrm{odd}}(G)}{2} . 1573: is a path (or cycle) odd-cover. For this strategy to succeed, it is necessary that  p(\mathcal{F})=1  in the path case and  p(\mathcal{F})=0  in the cycle case. Indeed, if  \mathcal{F}^{\prime}  is a path odd-cover, then it is also a linear forest odd-cover with odd parity, and it is easily seen that  p(\mathcal{F})=p(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}) . Similarly, if  \mathcal{F}^{\prime}  is a cycle odd-cover, then because it has no endpoints, each  M_{ij}  must be a perfect matching in  R_{ij}(\mathcal{F}) , and so we need  r_{ij}  to be even. For the path case, the condition  p(\mathcal{F})=1  also turns out to be sufficient, so our proof of Theorem 4.1 in Section 4.2 is rather short. For the cycle case, however, we will not only need that  p(\mathcal{F})=0 , but also that each  t_{i}(\mathcal{F})>0 ; otherwise, in some  F_{i} , we will have no way to join the components with endpoints in  R_{ij}  with those with endpoints in  R_{ik} .

## 369438: The coRE-hardness conjecture for distinguishing commuting quantum game values

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a game, and let $\omega_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{G})$ denote its commuting quantum value. Consider the promise problem in which

$$
\omega_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{G})=1\qquad\text{or}\qquad\omega_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathcal{G})\leq \frac{1}{4}.
$$

**CoRE-hardness conjecture.** Deciding which of these two cases holds is $\mathrm{coRE}$-hard; equivalently, $\mathrm{MIP}^{\mathrm{co}}=\mathrm{coRE}$.

This conjecture supplies the hardness premise needed to construct games whose finite-level NPA scores substantially exceed their true commuting quantum values. Its status is not resolved in the supplied source.

Primary locator: v1 Conjecture 4.2; Propositions 4.1 and 4.3

## 369455: TxGraffiti's harmonic-index bound for minimal maximal matchings

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a nontrivial connected graph. Let $\mu^*(G)$ denote the minimum cardinality of a maximal matching, and let $H(G)$ denote the harmonic index of $G$. **TxGraffiti's conjecture.** If $G$ is a nontrivial connected graph, then

$$
\mu^*(G)\leq H(G),
$$

and this bound is sharp. The conjecture links the degree-based harmonic index with a discrete edge-saturation parameter; the supplied text gives no resolution evidence.

Primary locator: Original Conjecture 4; Gupta arXiv2606.15761v3 Theorems 3-4

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

394: A third invariant introduced by Fajtlowicz Fajtlowicz (1988), called the residue  R(G) , is computed using the classical Havel–Hakimi process rather than by direct optimization. The Havel–Hakimi process entails repeatedly sorting a given integer sequence in decreasing order, removing the largest element  a_{1} , and subtracting 1 from the next  a_{1}  elements. The residue is defined as the number of zeros in the final sequence produced by the iterative application of the Havel–Hakimi process when starting from the degree sequence of  G . The Havel–Hakimi Theorem Hakimi (1962); Havel (1955) states that a sequence is graphic if and only if this process terminates in all zeros. Fajtlowicz, using his automated conjecturing program Graffiti Fajtlowicz (1990); DeLaViña (2005b), announced the conjecture  \alpha(G)\geq R(G) , a statement later proven in Favaron et al. (1991), and

Thus, Conjecture 1 holds for regular bipartite graphs. Next, recall that every König-Egerváry graph  G  of order  n  satisfies  \alpha(G)+\mu(G)=n . Furthermore, for cubic graphs  G  of order  n , where  n  is a multiple of four, it is true that  \alpha(G)\geq n/4 , while  a(G)=n/2 ,  R(G)=n/4 , and  \Delta=3 . Thus, Conjecture 1 also holds for cubic König-Egerváry graphs.

## 369604: Krause's ideal-theoretic reformulation of the telescope conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $\mathcal{D}$ be a compactly generated triangulated category, and let $\mathbf{t}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})$ be a smashing semiorthogonal decomposition of $\mathcal{D}$. Let $\mathcal{D}^c$ denote the compact objects, and let $I_{\mathbf{t}}$ be the ideal of $\mathcal{D}^c$ consisting of morphisms $\alpha\colon C\to C'$ such that

$$
\mathcal{D}(\alpha,Y)\colon\mathcal{D}(C',Y)\to\mathcal{D}(C,Y)
$$

is zero for every $Y\in\mathcal{Y}$.

**Krause's reformulated telescope conjecture.** The ideal $I_{\mathbf{t}}$ is generated by identity morphisms of objects in $\mathcal{D}^c$.

Krause's reformulation is equivalent to the compact-generation formulation because the decomposition is compactly generated precisely when its associated ideal is generated by such identities. It is refuted in the same generality as the telescope conjecture.

Primary locator: Section 8 telescope reformulation

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

63: 5 Approaching the Cuadra–Simson Problem via colocalization
70: 5.3 Reformulation of the Cuadra–Simson Problem and its refutation
75: 6 Partial affirmative answers to the Cuadra–Simson Problem
151: A problem raised by Cuadra and Simson in 2007 asks whether any locally finitely presented Grothendieck category with enough flat objects also has enough projectives. In this paper, we start from a key observation: a locally finitely presented Grothendieck category has enough flat objects if, and only if, it has exact products. This enables several equivalent reformulations of the problem, allowing us to identify a counterexample (thus providing a negative solution to the problem), while also connecting it to a classical ring-theoretical question posed by Miller in 1975, and even to the Telescope Conjecture for compactly generated triangulated categories. Moreover, we describe several classes of Grothendieck categories where the problem can be answered affirmatively. For example, we show that a locally finitely presented Grothendieck category whose category of finitely presented objects is Krull–Schmidt has enough flats if, and only if, it is generated by a family of finitely generated projectives.
182: Our main motivation comes from three problems raised by Juan Cuadra and Daniel Simson (see [19, Open Problems 2.9]). The first of them (Problem 2.9.(a)) asks for the characterization of Grothendieck categories as in the title. Motivated by the so-called Enoch’s or flat cover conjecture, whose settlement for module categories (see [9]) represented a major breakthrough in Ring Theory and later on in other areas of Mathematics (see, e.g., [51]), Problem 2.9.(b) of [19] asked whether any locally finitely presented Grothendieck category had flat covers. This problem was answered in the affirmative by Wolfgang Rump (see [66, Corollary 5.2] and also [18]), thus gathering in one several instances of those Grothendieck categories for which the flat cover conjecture had been proved (see [23, 24, 25]). Note that the existence of flat covers in such a Grothendieck category does not imply the existence of enough flat objects. The third problem [19, Problem 2.9.(c)] asks whether any Grothendieck category as in the title has enough projectives. This problem has remained open since then and, although there was a feeling that the answer would be negative (see [66, Section 6, Remark]), it still continues to call the attention of algebraists (see [15] for a very recent example).
192: Cuadra–Simson Problem.
203: The most important consequence of our results is a couple of reformulations of the problem (see Problem 5.11) that implies that such a  \mathcal{G}  is always a bilocalizing subcategory of a module category over a ring or over a small preadditive category, and that a locally finitely presented Grothendieck category has enough flat objects if, and only if, it is  \mathrm{AB}\mathchar 45\relax 4^{\ast}  (see Corollary 5.9). By these reformulations, we get two surprises. First, the problem is linked to some classical and some new problems that people have been studying for a while. Secondly, the problem has a negative answer in general. More specifically, we will show that the Cuadra–Simson Problem is both a particular case of a broader question by Robert Miller, who asked when an idempotent ideal of an (always associative and unital) ring is the trace of a projective module, and also equivalent to one of the questions posted online by Aurélien Djament (see [21]), who asked whether an  \mathrm{AB}\mathchar 45\relax 4^{\ast}  Grothendieck category must be generated by a family of finitely generated projectives, whenever it is either locally noetherian, locally coherent or, at least, locally finitely generated. In the final section of the paper, we will also establish a connection between the Cuadra–Simson Problem, and the Telescope Conjecture for general compactly generated triangulated categories. Concretely, if  \mathcal{D}  is such a triangulated category,  \mathcal{D}^{c}  is its subcategory of compact objects and  \mathbf{t}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})  is a smashing semiorthogonal decomposition  \mathcal{D} , then  \mathbf{t}  is compactly generated exactly when the bilocalizing subcategory of  \mathrm{Mod\mathchar 45\relax}\mathcal{D}^{c}  associated to the idempotent ideal  I_{\mathbf{t}}  of  \mathcal{D}^{c}  defined by Krause is locally finitely presented (see Theorem 8.4).
208: On what concerns the negative answer to the problem, we show that if  R  is a ring and  I  is an idempotent two-sided ideal of  R  that satisfies one of the conditions (i) and (ii) below, and it is not the trace of a projective right  R -module then  \mathcal{G}_{I} , the bilocalizing subcategory of  \mathrm{Mod\mathchar 45\relax}R  associated to  I , is locally finitely presented and has enough flat objects, but not enough projective objects (see Theorem 5.14 for an extended version):
230: In fact, following a suggestion by Herbera and Příhoda [32], we will show in Proposition 5.15 that, if  R  belongs to the class of Dubrovin–Puninski rings (studied in [22], and in the appendix of [6]), then it has a two-sided idempotent ideal  I  that satisfies both conditions (i) and (ii) above and, moreover, it is not the trace of a projective right  R -module, thus solving the Cuadra–Simson Problem in the negative.
236: But the negative solution of the general Cuadra–Simson Problem is not the end, as it is still interesting to study its restriction to suitable classes of categories. In this direction, we show that the problem has an affirmative solution when  \mathcal{G}  is a bilocalizing subcategory of the module category over: (i) a commutative ring (Theorem 6.1); (ii) a small additive category all of whose objects have semiregular endomorphism rings (Theorem 6.4). As a consequence of this last result, we get an affirmative answer when  \mathcal{G}  is locally finite, in particular when  \mathcal{G}=\mathrm{Comod\mathchar 45\relax}C  is the category of comodules over a  K -coalgebra, where  K  is a field (see Corollary 6.8). This was the problem that originally motivated Cuadra and Simson.
246: In Section 5 we find the desired reduction of the Cuadra–Simson Problem showing that if  \mathcal{G}  is locally finitely presented and has enough flat objects, then it is  \mathrm{AB}\mathchar 45\relax 6  and  \mathrm{AB}\mathchar 45\relax 4^{\ast}  (Proposition 5.3), which implies, by an old result of Roos, that there are a small preadditive category (even a ring)  \mathcal{A}  and an idempotent ideal  I  of  \mathcal{A}  such that  \mathcal{G}  is equivalent to  \mathcal{X}_{I} . We then study the concepts relevant for the Cuadra–Simson Problem within the category  \mathcal{X}_{I} , showing that this category always has enough flat objects, has enough projectives (resp., a set of finitely projective generators) exactly when  I  is the trace of a projective (resp., a set of finitely generated projective)  \mathcal{A} -modules and is locally finitely presented exactly when  \mathcal{C}_{I}  is generated by  \mathcal{X}_{I}\cap\mathrm{mod\mathchar 45\relax}\mathcal{A} , where  \mathrm{mod\mathchar 45\relax}\mathcal{A}  is the subcategory of finitely presented  \mathcal{A} -modules (Theorem 5.7). We end the section by explicitly reformulating the conjecture (see Problem 5.11) in two equivalent ways, something that we emphasize here in an equivalent way:

## 369626: Markov Uniqueness conjecture for Markov triples

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $(a,b,c)$ and $(a',b',c')$ be positive integer solutions of the Markov equation

$$
x^2+y^2+z^2=3xyz.
$$

**Markov Uniqueness conjecture.** If $\max(a,b,c)=\max(a',b',c')$, then there exists a permutation $\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_3$ such that

$$
(a,b,c)=\sigma(a',b',c').
$$

This century-old conjecture asserts uniqueness of a Markov triple up to permutation at each value of the maximum coordinate, and is still open.

Primary locator: Markov uniqueness in introduction

677: In Section 2, we define the cluster symmetric map (Definition 2.1) and prove the main conclusion (Theorem 2.19), which gives a method for constructing Laurent polynomials that are invariant under a given cluster symmetric map.
682: In Section 3, we recall some definitions and some results about generalized cluster algebra, discuss the relationship between generalized cluster algebras and cluster symmetric maps (Proposition 3.14), describe the invariant Laurent polynomial ring for some special cases (Proposition 3.21, Theorem 3.28), and answer two questions posed by Gyoda and Matsushita in [GM23] (Proposition 3.25, Proposition 3.31).
687: In Section 4, we discuss solutions to Diophantine equations related to cluster theory. We prove that the Markov-cluster equations possess the same solution structure as the Markov equation, that is, its set of positive integer solutions coincides exactly with a group orbit (Theorem 4.2).
692: In Section 5, we define cluster symmetric set of a Laurent polynomial (Definition 5.1), and give an algorithm to find cluster symmetry of a Laurent polynomial (Algorithm 5.5). We then determine when a Laurent polynomial corresponds to a generalized cluster algebra (Proposition 5.9). ## 369643: Backbone colouring conjecture for chordal graphs and spanning forests

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $G$ be a chordal graph and let $H$ be a spanning forest of $G$. Here $`\operatorname{BBC}_2(G,H)` denotes the minimum number of colours in a colouring of $G$ such that endpoints of every edge of $H$ receive colours differing by at least $2$, and $`\omega(G)` is the clique number of $G$.

**Backbone colouring conjecture.**

$$
\operatorname{BBC}_2(G,H)\leq \omega(G)+\mathcal O(1).
$$

The paper presents this as an open conjecture motivated by the bound $\omega(G)+\mathcal O(\sqrt{\omega(G)})$ proved for spanning forests. It predicts a constant additive term independent of the chordal graph and its clique number.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1; original Problem 2

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

235: Let  G=(V,E)  be a graph. Given a positive integer  k , we denote the set  \{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant k\}  by  [k] . A proper  k -colouring of  G  is a function  c:V(G)\rightarrow[k]  such that  c(u)\neq c(v)  holds for every edge  uv\in E(G) . We say  G  is  k -colourable if there exists a proper  k -colouring of  G . The chromatic number of  G , denoted by  \chi(G) , is the smallest  k  for which  G  is  k -colourable. We say  G  is  k -chromatic if  \chi(G)=k . The Vertex Colouring Problem consists of determining  \chi(G) , for a given graph  G . It is a well-known  {\sf NP} -hard problem [18] and one of the most studied problems on Graph Theory [17, 19].
240: The Vertex Colouring Problem models several practical applications, frequency assignment problems being perhaps the most famous ones [1]. There are several variations of the Vertex colouring Problem that were defined in order to model the specific constraints of the practical applications related to frequency assignment in networks. Broersma et al. [11, 10] defined the Backbone Colouring Problem to model the situation where certain channels of communication are more demanding than others.
245: Formally, given a graph  G , a spanning subgraph  H  of  G , called the backbone of  G , and two positive integers  q  and  k , a  q -backbone  k -colouring of  (G,H)  is a proper  k -colouring  c  of  G  for which  \lvert c(u)-c(v)\rvert\geqslant q  holds for every  uv\in E(H) . The  q -backbone chromatic number of  (G,H) , denoted by  \BBC_{q}(G,H) , is the minimum  k  for which there exists a  q -backbone  k -colouring of  (G,H) . The Backbone Colouring Problem consists of determining  \BBC_{q}(G,H)  [11, 10].
329: Let us consider the upper bound provided by (1) when  q=2 . If  G  is planar, by the Four Colour Theorem [2, 3] we have that  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant 7 . If  G  is a chordal graph, since it is perfect, we know that  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant 2\chi(G)-1=2\omega(G)-1 .
334: Problem 1 (​​ [11]).
343: Problem 2 (​​ [11]).
353: Concerning Problem 1, the authors also asked for a proof that  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant 7 , given a planar graph  G  and a spanning forest  H  of  G , without using the Four Colour Theorem.
354: Many authors in the literature considered that the answer to Problem 1 should be affirmative and it is treated as a conjecture by most of the related works. This conjecture would be tight even if  H  is a Hamiltonian path, as there are examples of a planar graph  G  and Hamiltonian path  H  in  G  for which  \BBC_{2}(G,H)=6  [12]. Problem 1 has also a version proposed by the same authors when  H  is restricted to be a matching, in which case the upper bound is conjectured to be 5 [12]. There is also another variation of Problem 1 for circular backbone colourings where the upper bound is 7 [16].
359: Some effort to solve Problem 1 and its variations has been done by different authors in the last decade. Campos et al. [13] confirmed that Problem 1 has a positive answer if  H  is a tree of diameter at most 4. For larger values of  q , Havet et al. [16] proved that, if  G  is planar and  H  is a forest in  G , then  \BBC_{q}(G,H)\leqslant q+6  and that this is tight for  q=4 . Regarding circular backbone colouring, Havet et al. [16] proved that if  G  is planar and  H  is a forest in  G , then  \CBC_{q}(G,H)\leqslant 2q+4  and conjectured that this bound is not tight. This was latter verified by [5], that also presented partial results for the circular variant of Problem 1. Araujo et al. [4] proved bounds when  G  is a planar graph without cycles of length 4 or 5 and  H  is a spanning forest of  G  in the circular variant of Problem 1.
364: On the other hand, Problem 2 was not much studied in the literature. In case  G  is chordal and  H  is a Hamiltonian path of  G , Broersma et al. [11] observed that  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant\chi(G)+4 . Actually, the technique they used can be easily extended to prove that if  G  is chordal and  H  has degree at most  c , then  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant\omega(G)+2c .
375: In this work, we present some partial results towards the resolution of Problem 2 by possibly constraining  G  to some subclasses of chordal graphs and also releasing the constraint of  H  to be a forest.
376: We first consider the case of  H  being bipartite. When  G  is an interval graph in which each vertex belongs to at most two maximal cliques, we give a positive answer to Problem 2.
380: Theorem 1.
390: We then show that Theorem 1 does not extend to the more general case of  G  being chordal by exhibiting a family of chordal graphs  G  with spanning bipartite subgraphs  H  satisfying  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\geqslant\frac{5}{3}\omega(G) .
410: Theorem 2.
420: We finally show that  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant\frac{3}{2}\omega(G)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)  holds whenever  G  is chordal and  H  is  C_{4} -free, which is thus halfway between Problem 2 and the trivial bound  \BBC_{2}(G,H)\leqslant 2\chi(G)-1 . Recall that  H  is  C_{4} -free if  H  does not contain  C_{4}  as a subgraph (not necessarily induced).
424: Theorem 3.
463: It is well-known that every graph  G  is a subgraph of a chordal graph  G^{\prime}  with  \omega(G^{\prime})={\sf tw}(G)+1  (see for instance [14, Corollary 12.3.12]). Hence the following is a direct consequence of Theorems 2 and 3.
529: The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 1 and Proposition 1.
556: Figure 1: Representation of sets used in the proof of Theorem 1
729: Figure 2: Colours’ ordering for vertices in  X_{i}  in the proof of Theorem 1
752: In the sequel, we show that the upper bound provided by Theorem 1 cannot be extended to the more general case of  G  being chordal.
790: This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem 2.
944: It is a direct consequence of Theorem 2 as  \Mad(T)<2  holds for any tree  T .
956: This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem 3.
975: In [7] (proof of Theorem 7), it was further shown that there exists a proper colouring  \varphi:V\rightarrow\{1,\cdots,\frac{\omega+3}{2}\}  such that, for each  t\in V(T) , every colour appears at most twice in  X_{t}  and at most three colours appear uniquely in  X_{t} . Moreover, using the fact that  H  is  C_{4} -free, two vertices that are adjacent in  H  are assigned distinct colours.

## 369669: Horizon-map determination of the set of minima

Literal MathDB statement:

For a set of minima, let $C_{\mathrm{max}}$ be the largest set of curves containing $C$ for which every nonfilling subset $C'$ of $C_{\mathrm{max}}$ gives $\mathrm{Min}(C')^{\infty}$ adherent to $\mathrm{Min}(C)$. The set $C_{\mathrm{max}}$ need not have the property that its curves intersect pairwise at most once. **Horizon-map determination conjecture.** The image of the horizon map uniquely determines the set of minima

$$
\mathrm{Min}(C)=\mathrm{Min}(C_{\mathrm{max}}).
$$

Intuitively, a set of minima behaves like a convex hull of the minima on its boundary, and the horizon map determines this boundary. The supplied text does not state whether the conjecture has been resolved.

Primary locator: Conjecture 3.5; horizon-map definitions

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

281: Theorem 1.1.
313: Theorem 1.2.
323: At a critical point described by Theorem 1.2, the assumptions of the theorem together with Proposition 1.3 of [11] imply that  \mathcal{P}_{g}  has a tangent space at  p  of dimension equal to the codimension of  \mathrm{Min}(C) . 
335: In the process of proving Theorems 1.1 and 1.2, the sets of curves that become arbitrarily short on a set of minima were completely determined. This gives a puncture-free analogue of the screens of Penner-McShane [19] and of a construction due to Harer [8]. As explained in Section 3, the thin parts of sets of minima have a combinatorial structure that can be identified with a subcomplex of the barycentric subdivision of Harvey’s curve complex. By analogy with punctured surfaces, it would seem that the horizon map uniquely determines a set of minima. If so, this implies the index of a critical point can be computed combinatorially under fairly general assumptions. Previously, except when the set of systoles at the critical point does not contain any proper filling subsets (see Section 3 of [11]) this could only be done numerically.
341: Outline of the paper. This paper is a sequel to [11], and relies on a a number of results about sets of minima, loci and the Thurston spine. These results are surveyed in Section 2 in an attempt at self-containment. Section 3 defines the horizon map and explains how to calculate it. The horizon map determines the asymptotic properties of the combinatorial description of sets of minima discussed in Section 4. Theorem 1.1 is also proven in this Section. Finally, in Section 5, Theorem 1.2 is proven as part of a discussion of what a dual to the Thurston spine should be, and how to construct duals from sets of minima.

This is a consequence of Theorem 2.3 and the existence of an upper bound, Bers’ constant, on  f_{\mathrm{sys}} , [4]. As all maxima of  f_{\mathrm{sys}}  are realised in the thick part of  \mathcal{T}_{g} , which is a compact modulo the action of  \Gamma_{g} , the existence of an upper bound for the  \Gamma_{g} -equivariant function  f_{\mathrm{sys}}  is a consequence of continuity.

For sufficiently small  \epsilon , a cell decomposition of a codimension 1 sphere of radius  \epsilon  centered on  p  inherits a cell decomposition from the stratification. One reason for being interested in duals is that they can be used to relate the topology of the boundary of the thick part of  \mathcal{T}_{g}  – an object that will be identified with Harvey’s curve complex by Theorem 2.4 – with  \Gamma_{g} -equivariant collapsibility properties of  \mathcal{P}_{g} .

This definition is  \Gamma_{g} -equivariant, and by Theorems 2.12 and 2.13, the dimension of  \mathrm{Min}(C)  is equal to the codimension of  \mathcal{P}_{g}  at  p . It follows that, by Theorem 1.2,  \mathrm{Min}(C)  has all the properties one would expect of a dual intersecting  \mathcal{P}_{g}  in the point  p .

This is nearly true; as illustrated in Figure 13, it is also necessary to know the systoles on the unbalanced strata in  \mathcal{P}_{g} . The sets of systoles on such unbalanced strata give sets of minima that act as connector pieces between the sets of minima  \{\mathrm{Min}(C_{1}),\mathrm{Min}(C_{2}),\ldots\} . The author is not aware of any examples of unbalanced strata in  \mathcal{P}_{g} , although they presumably exist. An algorithm is given in [23] for calculating all the local extrema of  f_{\mathrm{sys}}  and their systoles.

## 369690: The largest xeric open set conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $k$ be a number field and let $X$ be a smooth projective geometrically irreducible variety over $k$. Let $Z_{rc}(X)\subseteq X$ be the Zariski closure of the union of all possibly singular rational curves in $X_{k^{\mathrm{alg}}}$. **Largest xeric open set conjecture.** The open subset $X\setminus Z_{rc}(X)$ is xeric and hence is the largest xeric open set in $X$. This conjecture is suggested by Manin's rational curve conjecture and gives a geometric description of the maximal open locus where rational points are sparse. Its status is open.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1.5; xeric definition

## 369704: Induced-minor-free product structure conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $H$ be a graph. For a graph $G$, being **$H$-induced-minor-free** means that $G$ does not contain $H$ as an induced minor. Let $J\boxtimes P$ denote the strong product of graphs $J$ and $P$, and let $\operatorname{tw}(J)$ denote the treewidth of $J$.

**Induced-minor-free product structure conjecture.** There is a function $f\colon \mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}$ such that every $H$-induced-minor-free graph $G$ with maximum degree at most $\Delta$ is isomorphic to a subgraph of $J\boxtimes P$ for some graph $J$ with $\operatorname{tw}(J)\leqslant f(\Delta)$ and for some path $P$.

This is proposed as a common strengthening of product-structure results for planar and bounded-degree minor-free graphs. Its resolution is not given in the source.

Primary locator: Conjecture 23

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

261: Through a monumental series of 23 papers, Robertson and Seymour [44] established a rich theory of the structure of graphs defined by an excluded minor. Among their central results are the Excluded Grid Minor Theorem [45], which states that graphs that exclude a planar graph as a minor have bounded treewidth, and their celebrated Graph Minor Structure Theorem [46], which characterises graphs that exclude a non-planar graph as a minor.
266: Inspired by the success of this theory, a recent line of work aims to establish an analogous theory for graph classes defined by an excluded induced minor  H . To date, substantial progress has been made in the case when  H  is planar. For example, Korhonen [34] established the following induced version of the Excluded Grid Minor Theorem for bounded-degree graphs:

So it is natural to suspect that a similar result might hold for non-planar graphs, where grids are replaced by subdivided complete graphs. However, Korhonen and Lokshtanov [35] observed that this is false: the family of  (k\times k) -crossing grids has maximum degree at most  8 , excludes  K_{5}^{(1)}  as an induced minor, yet contains all graphs as minors (see Figure 1). This illustrates the challenge of formulating a structural conjecture for bounded-degree graphs that exclude a non-planar graph as an induced minor.

## 369705: Lokshtanov–McCarty bounded-degree region intersection conjecture

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $H$ be a graph and let $\Delta\in\mathbb{N}$. A **region intersection graph** over a graph $H'$ is a graph whose vertices correspond to connected subgraphs of $H'$, with two vertices adjacent exactly when the corresponding subgraphs share a vertex.

**Lokshtanov–McCarty's conjecture.** There exists a graph $H'$ such that every $H$-induced-minor-free graph with maximum degree at most $\Delta$ is a region intersection graph over an $H'$-minor-free graph.

This is the bounded-degree version of a conjecture independently attributed to Lokshtanov and McCarty. The unrestricted conjecture was recently disproved, while the bounded-degree version is proposed in the source as an open revival.

Primary locator: Conjecture 24

261: Through a monumental series of 23 papers, Robertson and Seymour [44] established a rich theory of the structure of graphs defined by an excluded minor. Among their central results are the Excluded Grid Minor Theorem [45], which states that graphs that exclude a planar graph as a minor have bounded treewidth, and their celebrated Graph Minor Structure Theorem [46], which characterises graphs that exclude a non-planar graph as a minor.

## 369770: Eventual negativity of averaged Ricci curvature in higher eigenspaces

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $V_\ell$ denote the eigenspace indexed by $\ell$ in the Zeitlin model on $SU(N)$, and let $\widetilde{r}_\ell(N)$ be the averaged Ricci curvature in that eigenspace. **Eventual negativity conjecture.** For each fixed $\ell>1$, $\widetilde{r}_\ell(N)$ becomes negative for sufficiently large $N$, and

$$
\widetilde{r}_\ell(N)\longrightarrow-\frac{H_\ell-1}{2}\quad\text{as }N\longrightarrow\infty,
$$

where $H_\ell$ is the $\ell^{\text{th}}$ harmonic number. Numerical tables and plots exhibit this sign transition and support the stated limit, but no proof or resolution is given.

Primary locator: Source Conjecture 2; Omega RicciLimit.claim/result

## 369811: Conjecture on the asymptotic injectivity of the difference-multiset map

Literal MathDB statement:

Let $A \subseteq \{0,1,2,\dots,n\}$ with $0 \in A$. Define $\phi$ on all such sets by sending $A$ to the multiset with $|A|^{2}$ elements given by

$$
A-A=\{ x-y:x,y\in A \}.
$$

**Difference-multiset injectivity conjecture.** As $n \to \infty$, the image of $\phi$ contains $2^{n-1}+o(2^{n})$ distinct elements. For a set $A$ with maximum element $a'$, the reflected set $A'=\{a'-a:a\in A\}$ has the same difference multiset, so the conjecture asserts that, asymptotically, a difference multiset almost always determines the pair $A$ and $A'$. Establishing this conjecture would imply that almost all relevant 0,1-polynomials have an irreducible non-reciprocal part. The paper presents it as an open problem associated with a lemma, and does not provide a resolution.

Primary locator: Conjecture 1; polynomial reciprocity discussion

Source context index (not a full-body reading claim):

A polynomial  f(x)\in\mathbb{R}[x]  is said to be reciprocal if  f(x)=\pm\tilde{f}(x) .
